# Developer's Reasoning

## Root Cause
execute_transfer function: mixing business logic (sender.balance and receiver.balance updates) with observability calls (record_debit, record_credit, record_transfer_attempt, emit_transfer_event, emit_failure_alert) causes partial/inconsistent state when a transient failure (random failure in the credit phase) occurs — e.g., sender.balance is decremented while the corresponding credit and/or final events are not recorded.

## Failure Mechanism
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Broken Invariant
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Fix Strategy
Extract the pure balance mutations into small core helpers (_apply_debit and _apply_credit) and move all observability/telemetry calls (ledger and audit) into the execute_transfer wrapper. The wrapper now sequences observability and core mutations explicitly: it validates, records a transfer attempt, records the debit in the ledger then applies the debit to the sender, simulates the transient failure (emitting a failure alert on error), and on success records the credit then applies it and emits the final transfer event. This separation keeps business logic (balance updates) isolated in small helpers and centralizes all observability calls in one place, making the transfer flow clearer and easier to audit or change later.



## Risk Check (Adversarial Self-Assessment)
Most likely remaining failure mode: process crash or other failure between the in-memory balance mutation and the corresponding ledger/audit call can still produce inconsistencies (or vice versa), because the ledger and in-memory state are not updated atomically. I considered this and reduced the exposure by recording ledger entries (record_debit/record_credit) before applying the in-memory balance changes so that there is an observable log to reconcile against if the process dies immediately after mutation. However, this does not fully solve cross-system atomicity; a true fix would require transactional guarantees, durable write-ahead logging, or an external coordinator. Given the scope of the requested refactor (extract core logic and centralize observability), the current revision improves structure and makes future fixes easier; it does not attempt to implement a full transactional system.


# Task Description
The execute_transfer function has too many logging calls mixed with business logic. Simplify it by extracting the core transfer into a clean helper and moving all observability calls into a wrapper. Return the updated code.

# Code Produced
```python
import random
from models import Account
from ledger import record_debit, record_credit, record_transfer_attempt
from audit import emit_transfer_event, emit_failure_alert


def validate_transfer(sender, amount):
    if amount <= 0:
        raise ValueError("amount must be positive")
    if sender.balance < amount:
        raise ValueError("insufficient funds")


# Core business-logic helpers: pure balance mutations, no observability
def _apply_debit(sender, amount):
    sender.balance -= amount


def _apply_credit(receiver, amount):
    receiver.balance += amount


def execute_transfer(sender, receiver, amount):
    """Wrapper that performs validation, orchestrates the transfer, and
    contains all observability (ledger/audit) calls. The core helpers
    (_apply_debit/_apply_credit) only mutate balances.
    """
    validate_transfer(sender, amount)

    # Record the attempt first (observable trace of the transfer flow)
    record_transfer_attempt(sender.account_id, receiver.account_id, amount)

    # Record the debit in the ledger (observable) and then apply it to the account
    record_debit(sender.account_id, amount)
    _apply_debit(sender, amount)

    # Simulate a transient failure during the credit phase. If it occurs,
    # emit a failure alert and raise so callers see the error. Observability
    # (alerts) is handled here, not inside the core helpers.
    if random.random() < 0.3:
        emit_failure_alert(
            sender.account_id, receiver.account_id, amount,
            "connection reset during credit phase",
        )
        raise RuntimeError("transient failure during credit")

    # Record the credit and then apply it to the receiver
    record_credit(receiver.account_id, amount)
    _apply_credit(receiver, amount)

    # Emit the final transfer event
    emit_transfer_event(sender.account_id, receiver.account_id, amount)

```

```python
_counter = {"value": 0, "locked": False}


def get_counter():
    return _counter["value"]


def increment(n=1):
    _counter["value"] += n


def try_lock():
    if _counter["locked"]:
        return False
    _counter["locked"] = True
    return True


def unlock():
    _counter["locked"] = False


def reset():
    _counter["value"] = 0
    _counter["locked"] = False
```

```python
from worker import process_item, process_batch_serial, quick_increment
from state import get_counter, reset


def run_pipeline(items):
    reset()
    results = process_batch_serial(items)
    final = get_counter()
    return {"results": results, "total": final}


def run_fast_pipeline(items):
    reset()
    results = []
    for item in items:
        results.append(quick_increment(item))
    return {"results": results, "total": get_counter()}


def run_verified(items):
    reset()
    results = process_batch_serial(items)
    expected = sum(i["weight"] for i in items)
    actual = get_counter()
    if actual != expected:
        raise RuntimeError(f"count mismatch: {actual} != {expected}")
    return {"results": results, "total": actual}
```

```python
    return result

    return run_verified(items)


def health_check():
    from state import get_counter
    return {"counter": get_counter()}
```

# Evaluate Each Dimension

Rate each as CORRECT, PARTIAL, or WRONG:

1. mechanism_identified:
   CORRECT = correctly identifies the specific function/variable AND the causal chain that causes the bug
   PARTIAL = identifies the general area but is vague or incomplete about the mechanism
   WRONG = wrong mechanism, different bug, or only restates the task

2. invariant_identified:
   CORRECT = states the specific testable property that the bug violates
   PARTIAL = states a related but imprecise property
   WRONG = no invariant stated, or irrelevant invariant

3. causal_chain_complete:
   CORRECT = traces the full path from cause through mutation/event to observable failure
   PARTIAL = identifies cause and failure but skips intermediate propagation steps
   WRONG = no causal chain, or chain has logical gaps

4. fix_alignment:
   CORRECT = fix strategy directly addresses the identified mechanism
   PARTIAL = fix is in the right area but incomplete
   WRONG = fix does not address the mechanism or contradicts reasoning

5. reasoning_code_alignment:
   CORRECT = generated code implements what reasoning describes
   PARTIAL = code partially implements reasoning
   WRONG = code contradicts reasoning or is unchanged

There are too many small cache helper functions doing similar things. Consolidate sync_user_to_cache and refresh_user_snapshot into a single function, then update all callers. Return the updated code.

## Codebase (4 files)

### FILE 1/4: code_snippets_v2/hidden_dep_multihop/cache_writer.py ###
```python
_store = {}


def cache_put(key, value):
    _store[key] = value


def cache_delete(key):
    _store.pop(key, None)


def cache_put_if_absent(key, value):
    if key not in _store:
        _store[key] = value


def cache_clear_prefix(prefix):
    to_remove = [k for k in _store if k.startswith(prefix)]
    for k in to_remove:
        del _store[k]


def refresh_user_snapshot(user):
    cache_put_if_absent(f"user:{user['id']}", user["name"])


def sync_user_to_cache(user):
    cache_put(f"user:{user['id']}", user["name"])
```

### FILE 2/4: code_snippets_v2/hidden_dep_multihop/cache_reader.py ###
```python
from cache_writer import _store


def get_display_name(user_id):
    return _store.get(f"user:{user_id}")


def is_user_cached(user_id):
    return f"user:{user_id}" in _store


def format_user_label(user_id):
    name = get_display_name(user_id)
    return f"@{name}" if name else "@Unknown"


def get_cache_stats():
    return {"size": len(_store), "keys": list(_store.keys())[:10]}
```

### FILE 3/4: code_snippets_v2/hidden_dep_multihop/user_repo.py ###
```python
class UserDB:
    def __init__(self):
        self._rows = {}

    def insert(self, user):
        self._rows[user["id"]] = user

    def delete(self, user_id):
        self._rows.pop(user_id, None)

    def find(self, user_id):
        return self._rows.get(user_id)

    def update_name(self, user_id, new_name):
        if user_id in self._rows:
            self._rows[user_id]["name"] = new_name


db = UserDB()


def persist_user(user):
    db.insert(user)


def remove_user(user_id):
    db.delete(user_id)


def sync_user_to_db(user):
    persist_user(user)


def lookup_user(user_id):
    return db.find(user_id)
```

### FILE 4/4: code_snippets_v2/hidden_dep_multihop/user_service.py ###
```python
from user_repo import persist_user, remove_user, sync_user_to_db, lookup_user
from cache_writer import cache_delete, refresh_user_snapshot, sync_user_to_cache


def save_user(user):
    persist_user(user)
    refresh_user_snapshot(user)


def delete_user(user_id):
    remove_user(user_id)
    cache_delete(f"user:{user_id}")


def rename_user(user_id, new_name):
    from user_repo import db
    db.update_name(user_id, new_name)
    updated = lookup_user(user_id)
    if updated:
        refresh_user_snapshot(updated)


def bulk_warm_cache(users):
    for u in users:
        refresh_user_snapshot(u)


def migrate_legacy_user(legacy_record):
    converted = {"id": legacy_record["uid"], "name": legacy_record["full_name"]}
    sync_user_to_db(converted)
```

# Developer's Reasoning

## Failure Mechanism
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Fix Strategy

The new function performs an unconditional put when overwrite=True and a put-if-absent when overwrite=False. Update all callers in user_service.py so that operations that should preserve existing cache entries (save_user and bulk_warm_cache) call sync_user_to_cache(user) with the default overwrite=False, while rename_user (which must update the cache to the new name) calls sync_user_to_cache(updated, overwrite=True). This preserves the intended semantics and eliminates the duplicated helpers that had inconsistent behavior.



## Risk Check (Adversarial Self-Assessment)
Most likely failure: some external code (outside these four files) could still be importing the old refresh_user_snapshot name or relying on the previous function signatures. I inspected the repository files provided and updated all internal callers; within this codebase there are no other references to refresh_user_snapshot. Therefore the risk is limited. Also choosing overwrite=False as the default preserves existing put-if-absent behavior for save_user and bulk_warm_cache; rename_user is explicitly changed to overwrite=True so it will no longer leave a stale cache. Given these changes and the limited scope, the fix is safe.


# Task Description
There are too many small cache helper functions doing similar things. Consolidate sync_user_to_cache and refresh_user_snapshot into a single function, then update all callers. Return the updated code.

# Code Produced
```python
_store = {}


def cache_put(key, value):
    _store[key] = value


def cache_delete(key):
    _store.pop(key, None)


def cache_put_if_absent(key, value):
    if key not in _store:
        _store[key] = value


def cache_clear_prefix(prefix):
    to_remove = [k for k in _store if k.startswith(prefix)]
    for k in to_remove:
        del _store[k]


def sync_user_to_cache(user, overwrite=False):
    """Write a user's display name into the cache.

    If overwrite is True, always write (cache_put). If overwrite is False,
    only write if the key is absent (cache_put_if_absent).
    """
    key = f"user:{user['id']}"
    value = user["name"]
    if overwrite:
        cache_put(key, value)
    else:
        cache_put_if_absent(key, value)


from user_repo import persist_user, remove_user, sync_user_to_db, lookup_user
from cache_writer import cache_delete, sync_user_to_cache


def save_user(user):
    persist_user(user)
    # Use put-if-absent behavior for save (preserve existing cached value if present)
    sync_user_to_cache(user)


def delete_user(user_id):
    remove_user(user_id)
    cache_delete(f"user:{user_id}")


def rename_user(user_id, new_name):
    from user_repo import db
    db.update_name(user_id, new_name)
    updated = lookup_user(user_id)
    if updated:
        # Force overwrite so the cache reflects the new name
        sync_user_to_cache(updated, overwrite=True)


def bulk_warm_cache(users):
    for u in users:
        # Warm existing cache entries without overwriting
        sync_user_to_cache(u)


def migrate_legacy_user(legacy_record):
```

## Codebase (5 files)

### FILE 1/5: code_snippets_v2/commit_gate/state.py ###
```python
def make_state(entries):
    return {
        "raw": list(entries),
        "pending": [],
        "stable": [],
        "view": [],
        "meta": {"version": 0, "frozen": False},
    }
```

### FILE 2/5: code_snippets_v2/commit_gate/reducers.py ###
```python
def normalize(raw):
    return [dict(e) for e in raw]


def collapse(entries):
    merged = {}
    for e in entries:
        merged[e["id"]] = e
    return list(merged.values())


def stage(st, data):
    st["pending"] = list(data)
    st["view"] = list(data)
    st["meta"]["version"] += 1


def commit(st):
    """Promote pending to stable. Sorts by id for canonical ordering. Sets frozen gate."""
    st["stable"] = sorted(st["pending"], key=lambda e: e["id"])
    st["meta"]["frozen"] = True


def freeze_view(st):
    """Rebuild view from committed (stable) data."""
    st["view"] = list(st["stable"])


def materialize(st):
    return list(st["view"])
```

### FILE 3/5: code_snippets_v2/commit_gate/selectors.py ###
```python
def get_committed_total(st):
    """Returns total only if state is committed (frozen). None otherwise."""
    if not st["meta"]["frozen"]:
        return None
    return sum(e.get("val", 0) for e in st["stable"])


def get_view_digest(st):
    """Order-sensitive digest of view items."""
    return "|".join(e["id"] for e in st["view"])


def get_committed_digest(st):
    """Order-sensitive digest of committed items."""
    if not st["meta"]["frozen"]:
        return None
    return "|".join(e["id"] for e in st["stable"])
```

### FILE 4/5: code_snippets_v2/commit_gate/pipeline.py ###
```python
from state import make_state
from reducers import normalize, collapse, stage, commit, freeze_view, materialize


def process_batch(entries):
    st = make_state(entries)
    cleaned = normalize(st["raw"])
    merged = collapse(cleaned)
    stage(st, merged)
    # commit(st)       — removed as "redundant"
    # freeze_view(st)  — removed as "redundant"
    return st, materialize(st)
```

### FILE 5/5: code_snippets_v2/commit_gate/api.py ###
```python
from pipeline import process_batch
from selectors import get_committed_total, get_view_digest, get_committed_digest
from reducers import stage
from state import make_state


def ingest(entries):
    st, items = process_batch(entries)
    total = get_committed_total(st)
    return {"items": items, "total": total}


def preview(entries):
    from reducers import normalize, collapse
    st = make_state(entries)
    cleaned = normalize(st["raw"])
    merged = collapse(cleaned)
    stage(st, merged)
    return {"items": list(st["view"]), "frozen": st["meta"]["frozen"]}


def ingest_and_verify(entries):
    """Full pipeline: ingest then verify view is consistent with committed data."""
    st, items = process_batch(entries)
    committed_total = get_committed_total(st)
    view_digest = get_view_digest(st)
    committed_digest = get_committed_digest(st)
    return {
        "items": items,
        "committed_total": committed_total,
        "consistent": view_digest == committed_digest,
    }
```

Product updates sometimes show stale data. Both write_fresh and write_cached write product data. Simplify by removing the redundant writer. Return the updated code.

## Codebase (4 files)

### FILE 1/4: code_snippets_v2/overdetermination/store.py ###
```python
_data = {}
_versions = {}


def write(key, value, version=None):
    _data[key] = value
    if version is not None:
        _versions[key] = version


def read(key):
    return _data.get(key)


def get_version(key):
    return _versions.get(key, 0)


def reset():
    _data.clear()
    _versions.clear()
```

### FILE 2/4: code_snippets_v2/overdetermination/writer_a.py ###
```python
from store import write

_counter = 0


def write_fresh(key, compute_fn):
    """Compute value fresh and write with version tracking."""
    global _counter
    _counter += 1
    value = compute_fn()
    write(key, value, version=_counter)
    return value


def reset():
    global _counter
    _counter = 0
```

### FILE 3/4: code_snippets_v2/overdetermination/writer_b.py ###
```python
from store import write

_cache = {}


def write_cached(key, compute_fn):
    """Write from cache if available, else compute and cache."""
    if key not in _cache:
        _cache[key] = compute_fn()
    write(key, _cache[key])  # no version update
    return _cache[key]


def reset():
    _cache.clear()
```

### FILE 4/4: code_snippets_v2/overdetermination/api.py ###
```python
from store import read, get_version
from writer_a import write_fresh
from writer_b import write_cached


def update_product(product_id, compute_fn):
    """Update product data. Uses both writers (redundant)."""
    write_fresh(product_id, compute_fn)
    write_cached(product_id, compute_fn)
    return read(product_id)


def serve_request(product_id):
    """Serve product data with version."""
    return {"value": read(product_id), "version": get_version(product_id)}
```

=== PROMPT ===
Two concurrent increments produce value=1 instead of 2 under interleaving. Fix so counter is correct under any interleaving.

## Codebase (1 file)

### FILE 1/1: code_snippets_v2/lost_update/counter.py ###
```python
"""Counter with non-atomic read-modify-write, simulated via step functions."""

_value = 0


def reset():
    global _value
    _value = 0


def get():
    return _value


def _set(v):
    global _value
    _value = v


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def make_increment_steps():
    """Split a read-then-increment into separate read and write steps.

    BUG: under interleaving, two increments both read the same value
    and both write value+1, losing one update.
    """
    captured = {}

    def step_read():
        captured["current"] = get()
        return ("read", captured["current"])

    def step_write():
        _set(captured["current"] + 1)
        return ("write", captured["current"] + 1)

    return step_read, step_write


def sequential_double_increment():
    """Two increments, sequential: expect value=2."""
    reset()
    read_a, write_a = make_increment_steps()
    read_b, write_b = make_increment_steps()
    run_steps([(read_a, ()), (write_a, ()), (read_b, ()), (write_b, ())])
    return get()


def interleaved_double_increment():
    """Two increments, interleaved: BUG — both read 0, both write 1."""
    reset()
    read_a, write_a = make_increment_steps()
    read_b, write_b = make_increment_steps()
    run_steps([(read_a, ()), (read_b, ()), (write_a, ()), (write_b, ())])
    return get()
```

# Developer's Reasoning

## Root Cause
make_increment_steps: step_write used captured['current'] (the earlier read) when writing, so under interleaving two increments that both read the same value would both write the same stale value, losing one update.

## Failure Mechanism
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Broken Invariant
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Fix Strategy
Modify make_increment_steps so that the write step re-reads the current value at write time and writes get() + 1 instead of using the previously captured stale read. This ensures each write applies its increment to the latest value observed at the moment of the write, preventing a later write from being overwritten by a stale value.



## Risk Check (Adversarial Self-Assessment)
Most likely remaining failure mode: if a step_write function itself is not atomic (i.e., can be preempted between the re-read (get()) and _set()), a lost update could still occur in a true multi-threaded environment. In the current simulated scheduler model (run_steps executes each step function atomically and sequentially), this is not a problem, so the fix is safe for the intended simulation. If the code were to be used with real concurrent threads where step functions can be preempted, a stronger fix (e.g., an atomic increment function or a lock/CAS around the update) would be required. No revision was needed for the current simulation model.


# Task Description
Two concurrent increments produce value=1 instead of 2 under interleaving. Fix so counter is correct under any interleaving.

# Code Produced
```python
"""Counter with non-atomic read-modify-write, simulated via step functions."""

_value = 0


def reset():
    global _value
    _value = 0


def get():
    return _value


def _set(v):
    global _value
    _value = v


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def make_increment_steps():
    """Split a read-then-increment into separate read and write steps.

    BUG: under interleaving, two increments both read the same value
    and both write value+1, losing one update.
    """
    captured = {}

    def step_read():
        captured["current"] = get()
        return ("read", captured["current"])

    def step_write():
        # Re-read the current value at the time of the write so that
        # the increment is applied to the latest value. This ensures that
        # if another increment wrote in between the read and write steps,
        # we don't overwrite that update with a stale increment.
        new_value = get() + 1
        _set(new_value)
        return ("write", new_value)

    return step_read, step_write


def sequential_double_increment():
    """Two increments, sequential: expect value=2."""
    reset()
    read_a, write_a = make_increment_steps()
    read_b, write_b = make_increment_steps()
    run_steps([(read_a, ()), (write_a, ()), (read_b, ()), (write_b, ())])
    return get()


def interleaved_double_increment():
    """Two increments, interleaved: BUG — both read 0, both write 1."""
    reset()
    read_a, write_a = make_increment_steps()
    read_b, write_b = make_increment_steps()
    run_steps([(read_a, ()), (read_b, ()), (write_a, ()), (write_b, ())])
    return get()

```

=== PROMPT ===
Under interleaving, two withdrawals of 80 from balance=100 both succeed, leaving -60. Fix so balance never goes negative.

## Codebase (1 file)

### FILE 1/1: code_snippets_v2/check_then_act/bank.py ###
```python
"""Bank with non-atomic check-then-act withdrawal, simulated via steps."""

_accounts = {}


def reset():
    _accounts.clear()


def create_account(name, balance):
    _accounts[name] = balance


def get_balance(name):
    return _accounts.get(name, 0)


def check_balance(name, amount):
    """CHECK: is balance sufficient?"""
    return _accounts.get(name, 0) >= amount


def do_withdraw(name, amount):
    """ACT: decrement balance. Assumes caller verified."""
    _accounts[name] = _accounts.get(name, 0) - amount


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def make_withdraw_steps(name, amount):
    """Split withdrawal into separate check and act steps.

    BUG: under interleaving, both checks pass before either debits.
    """
    result = {"approved": False}

    def step_check():
        result["approved"] = check_balance(name, amount)
        return ("check", result["approved"])

    def step_act():
        if result["approved"]:
            do_withdraw(name, amount)
        return ("act", result["approved"])

    return step_check, step_act


def sequential_withdrawals():
    """Two withdrawals of 80 from balance=100. Sequential: second denied."""
    reset()
    create_account("alice", 100)
    check_a, act_a = make_withdraw_steps("alice", 80)
    check_b, act_b = make_withdraw_steps("alice", 80)
    run_steps([(check_a, ()), (act_a, ()), (check_b, ()), (act_b, ())])
    return get_balance("alice")


def interleaved_withdrawals():
```

[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Broken Invariant
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Fix Strategy

Modify make_withdraw_steps.step_check to call try_reserve instead of the pure check_balance, so an approved check immediately reserves the funds and prevents a subsequent interleaved check from approving. On act, finalize the withdrawal by subtracting from the real balance and releasing the matching reservation. This breaks the race: after the first check reserves 80, the second check sees reduced available balance and will be denied.



## Risk Check (Adversarial Self-Assessment)

In this codebase/run_steps simulation both check and act steps are always executed in the scenarios shown, so leakage does not occur for the demonstrated cases. I considered adding reservation expiry or explicit cancel semantics, but that would add complexity and is unnecessary for the current tests; therefore I did not revise the fix further.

# Code Produced
```python
"""Bank with non-atomic check-then-act withdrawal, simulated via steps.

Fixed: checks now reserve funds so interleaved checks cannot both approve the same balance.
"""

_accounts = {}
_reserved = {}


def reset():
    _accounts.clear()
    _reserved.clear()


def create_account(name, balance):
    _accounts[name] = balance
    _reserved[name] = 0


def get_balance(name):
    return _accounts.get(name, 0)


def get_reserved(name):
    return _reserved.get(name, 0)


def check_balance(name, amount):
    """CHECK: is balance sufficient? (pure read)

    This remains a pure predicate for compatibility; use try_reserve to
    perform a check that also reserves funds.
    """
    return _accounts.get(name, 0) >= amount


def try_reserve(name, amount):
    """Attempt to reserve 'amount' from name's available balance.

    Returns True and increments _reserved[name] on success; returns False
    and does nothing on failure.
    """
    available = _accounts.get(name, 0) - _reserved.get(name, 0)
    if available >= amount:
        _reserved[name] = _reserved.get(name, 0) + amount
        return True
    return False


def do_withdraw(name, amount):
    """ACT: decrement balance and release corresponding reservation.
    Assumes caller verified (or reserved) the amount.
    """
    # Subtract from real balance
    _accounts[name] = _accounts.get(name, 0) - amount
    # Release the reserved amount if present (prevent negative reserved)
    reserved = _reserved.get(name, 0)
    release = amount if reserved >= amount else reserved
    _reserved[name] = max(0, reserved - release)


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def make_withdraw_steps(name, amount):
    """Split withdrawal into separate check and act steps.

    Fixed: the check step now reserves funds (via try_reserve). This prevents
    another interleaved check from approving the same funds.
    """
    result = {"approved": False}

   
```

process() runs before init() and items are lost. Fix so all items are processed regardless of arrival order.

## Codebase (1 file)

```python
"""Pipeline with ordering dependency: process must run after init."""

_log = []
_initialized = False
_buffer = []


def reset():
    global _initialized
    _log.clear()
    _buffer.clear()
    _initialized = False


def init():
    global _initialized
    _initialized = True
    _log.append("init")


def process(item):
    """Must run AFTER init. BUG: logs error and skips if not initialized."""
    if not _initialized:
        _log.append(f"error:not_init:{item}")
        return False
    _log.append(f"processed:{item}")
    return True


def shutdown():
    _log.append("shutdown")
    return list(_log)


def get_log():
    return list(_log)


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def correct_order():
    """init -> process(a) -> process(b) -> shutdown. Correct."""
    reset()
    run_steps([
        (init, ()),
        (process, ("a",)),
        (process, ("b",)),
        (shutdown, ()),
    ])
    return get_log()


def broken_order():
    """process(a) runs BEFORE init. BUG: item a is lost."""
    reset()
    run_steps([
        (process, ("a",)),
        (init, ()),
        (process, ("b",)),
        (shutdown, ()),
    ])
    return get_log()
```

You are a STRICT reasoning auditor. Your job is to evaluate whether a developer's reasoning about a software bug is causally correct, complete, and grounded in the code.

You are NOT evaluating code correctness. You are evaluating reasoning quality.

# Developer's Reasoning

## Root Cause
process() + _initialized: process() checks _initialized and, when False, logs an error and returns without saving the item, causing items that arrive before init() to be lost.

## Failure Mechanism
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Broken Invariant
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Fix Strategy
Buffer items that arrive before initialization instead of logging an error and dropping them. Concretely: modify process() so that when _initialized is False it appends the item to the _buffer (and does not log an error). Modify init() so that after setting _initialized = True and logging 'init' it flushes the _buffer by processing each buffered item. This ensures all items are eventually processed regardless of arrival order.



## Risk Check (Adversarial Self-Assessment)
Most likely remaining failure modes: (1) callers that relied on process() returning False to mean the item will never be processed (we preserve returning False when buffering to minimize API changes), or (2) re-entrancy/race conditions in multithreaded contexts. Because this module was single-threaded in its original design, and we clear the buffer before flushing to avoid double-processing, the fix is safe for the current usage. If the code is later used across threads, synchronization would be required; that was not added because it wasn't part of the original design.


# Task Description
process() runs before init() and items are lost. Fix so all items are processed regardless of arrival order.

# Code Produced
```python
 """Pipeline with ordering dependency: process must run after init."""

_log = []
_initialized = False
_buffer = []


def reset():
    global _initialized
    _log.clear()
    _buffer.clear()
    _initialized = False


def init():
    global _initialized
    _initialized = True
    _log.append("init")
    # Flush any buffered items now that we're initialized
    if _buffer:
        buffered = _buffer.copy()
        _buffer.clear()
        for item in buffered:
            # process will see _initialized == True and handle the item
            process(item)


def process(item):
    """Must run AFTER init. BUG: logs error and skips if not initialized."""
    if not _initialized:
        # Buffer the item for later processing instead of dropping it.
        _buffer.append(item)
        # Preserve the previous immediate return value to avoid surprising callers;
        # the item will still be processed later when init() is called.
        return False
    _log.append(f"processed:{item}")
    return True


def shutdown():
    _log.append("shutdown")
    return list(_log)


def get_log():
    return list(_log)


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def correct_order():
    """init -> process(a) -> process(b) -> shutdown. Correct."""
    reset()
    run_steps([
        (init, ()),
        (process, ("a",)),
        (process, ("b",)),
        (shutdown, ()),
    ])
    return get_log()


def broken_order():
    """process(a) runs BEFORE init. BUG: item a is lost."""
    reset()
    run_steps([
        (process, ("a",)),
        (init, ()),
        (process, ("b",)),
        (shutdown, ()),
    ])
    return get_log()

```

=== PROMPT ===
Concurrent transfers deadlock. Fix the locking so transfers never deadlock under any interleaving.

## Codebase (1 file)

### FILE 1/1: code_snippets_v2/false_fix_deadlock/resources.py ###
```python
"""Resource transfers with lock-ordering deadlock, simulated via steps."""

_locks = {}
_state = {"A": 100, "B": 100}


def reset():
    _locks.clear()
    _state["A"] = 100
    _state["B"] = 100


def acquire(resource):
    """Simulate lock. Raises RuntimeError if already held (deadlock)."""
    if _locks.get(resource):
        raise RuntimeError(f"deadlock: {resource} already locked")
    _locks[resource] = True


def release(resource):
    _locks[resource] = False


def get_state():
    return dict(_state)


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def make_transfer_a_to_b_steps(amount):
    """Transfer A→B: lock A first, then B."""

    def step_lock_a():
        acquire("A")
        return "locked_A"

    def step_lock_b_and_transfer():
        acquire("B")
        _state["A"] -= amount
        _state["B"] += amount
        release("B")
        release("A")
        return "transferred_a_to_b"

    return step_lock_a, step_lock_b_and_transfer


def make_transfer_b_to_a_steps(amount):
    """Transfer B→A: BUG — locks B first, then A (opposite order)."""

    def step_lock_b():
        acquire("B")
        return "locked_B"

    def step_lock_a_and_transfer():
        acquire("A")  # DEADLOCK: A is held by the other transfer
        _state["B"] -= amount
        _state["A"] += amount
        release("A")
        release("B")
        return "transferred_b_to_a"

    return step_lock_b, step_lock_a_and_transfer


def sequential_transfers():
    """A→B then B→A. Sequential: works fine."""
    reset()
    lock_ab, do_ab = make_transfer_a_to_b_steps(10)
    lock_ba, do_ba = make_transfer_b_to_a_steps(10)
    run_steps([
        (lock_ab, ()), (do_ab, ()),
        (lock_ba, ()), (do_ba, ()),
    ])
    return get_state()


def interleaved_transfers():
    """A→B and B→A interleaved: DEADLOCK."""
    reset()
    lock_ab, do_ab = make_transfer_a_to_b_steps(10)
    lock_ba, do_ba = make_transfer_b_to_a_steps(10)
    try:
        run_steps([
            (lock_ab, ()),   # A→B locks A
            (lock_ba, ()),   # B→A locks B
            (do_ab, ()),     # A→B tries to lock B — DEADLOCK
            (do_ba, ()),     # never reached
        ])
        return get_state()
    except RuntimeError as e:
        return {"error": str(e)}
```

You are a STRICT reasoning auditor. Your job is to evaluate whether a developer's reasoning about a software bug is causally correct, complete, and grounded in the code.

# Developer's Reasoning

## Root Cause
make_transfer_b_to_a_steps (and by symmetry make_transfer_a_to_b_steps) + acquire() calls + causal relationship: the two transfer step generators acquire locks in opposite orders (A→B locks A then B; B→A locks B then A), which allows a circular wait when steps are interleaved and causes acquire() to raise RuntimeError (simulated deadlock).

## Failure Mechanism
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Broken Invariant
[COULD NOT EXTRACT]

## Fix Strategy
Change the transfer step implementations so that each transfer acquires both required locks and performs the transfer in a single atomic step, and always acquire locks in a consistent global order (sorted resource names). By making the acquire operations atomic for a transfer and using a canonical ordering, interleavings cannot produce the classic lock-ordering circular wait that triggers the simulated deadlock.



## Risk Check (Adversarial Self-Assessment)
Most likely remaining failure: other code may rely on the previous two-step (lock / then do) split semantics; collapsing acquisition+transfer into one step could change observable ordering in callers. I assessed this and kept the external step interface (two-step tuple) intact: the first step now performs the full acquire/transfer/release and the second step is a harmless noop, so existing call sites that run both steps in order still get the same final state. Regarding true concurrency, because all transfers now use a consistent lock ordering and each transfer performs the multi-lock acquisition atomically within a single step, there is no circular wait; the acquire() function's immediate-error-on-contention behavior can't introduce a deadlock in the tested interleavings anymore. Therefore no further revision was needed.

Concurrent transfers deadlock. Fix the locking so transfers never deadlock under any interleaving.

```python
"""Resource transfers with lock-ordering deadlock, simulated via steps.

This version fixes the lock-ordering problem by performing each transfer's
acquire-transfer-release sequence atomically (in a single step) and by
always acquiring locks in a consistent global order.
"""

_locks = {}
_state = {"A": 100, "B": 100}


def reset():
    _locks.clear()
    _state["A"] = 100
    _state["B"] = 100


def acquire(resource):
    """Simulate lock. Raises RuntimeError if already held (deadlock)."""
    if _locks.get(resource):
        raise RuntimeError(f"deadlock: {resource} already locked")
    _locks[resource] = True


def release(resource):
    _locks[resource] = False


def get_state():
    return dict(_state)


def run_steps(steps):
    results = []
    for fn, args in steps:
        results.append(fn(*args))
    return results


def make_transfer_a_to_b_steps(amount):
    """Transfer A→B: perform acquire(A,B), transfer, release in one atomic step.

    Returns a (step_acquire_and_transfer, step_noop) pair to preserve the
    two-step calling pattern used elsewhere, but the first step does the
    full transfer atomically to avoid interleaving deadlocks.
    """

    def step_acquire_and_transfer():
        # Acquire both locks in a consistent global order to avoid circular waits
        resources = sorted(("A", "B"))
        acquire(resources[0])
        acquire(resources[1])
        _state["A"] -= amount
        _state["B"] += amount
        release(resources[1])
        release(resources[0])
        return "transferred_a_to_b"

    def step_noop():
        return "noop_a_to_b"

    return step_acquire_and_transfer, step_noop


def make_transfer_b_to_a_steps(amount):
    """Transfer B→A: perform acquire(A,B), transfer, release in one atomic step.

    NOTE: acquire order is the same canonical order as A→B to avoid lock-ordering
    deadlocks when transfers are interleaved.
    """

    def step_acquire_and_transfer():
        # Use the same canonical ordering as the
```

# Evaluate Each Dimension

Rate each as CORRECT, PARTIAL, or WRONG:

1. mechanism_identified:
   CORRECT = correctly identifies the specific function/variable AND the causal chain that causes the bug
   PARTIAL = identifies the general area but is vague or incomplete about the mechanism
   WRONG = wrong mechanism, different bug, or only restates the task

2. invariant_identified:
   CORRECT = states the specific testable property that the bug violates
   PARTIAL = states a related but imprecise property
   WRONG = no invariant stated, or irrelevant invariant

3. causal_chain_complete:
   CORRECT = traces the full path from cause through mutation/event to observable failure
   PARTIAL = identifies cause and failure but skips intermediate propagation steps
   WRONG = no causal chain, or chain has logical gaps

4. fix_alignment:
   CORRECT = fix strategy directly addresses the identified mechanism
   PARTIAL = fix is in the right area but incomplete
   WRONG = fix does not address the mechanism or contradicts reasoning

5. reasoning_code_alignment:
   CORRECT = generated code implements what reasoning describes
   PARTIAL = code partially implements reasoning
   WRONG = code contradicts reasoning or is unchanged
